Document 003: 10月宏观数据点评：内外需延续分化格局

### Page 1

请务必阅读正文之后的免责条款部分 研究专业创新 如意投资随心
宏观研究
宏观点评
内外需延续分化格局
——10月宏观数据点评
事件：2026 年 11 月 16日，国家统计局发布 10 月份国内宏观经济数据。10
月份，全国规模以上工业增加值同比增长 4.8%，社会消费品零售总额同比增
长 0.4%；1—10 月全国固定资产投资（不含农户）同比下降 8.1%，房地产
开发投资同比下降 21.6%。
核心观点：
 10 月国内经济内外需分化格局延续，“供强需弱”特征进一步显现。各
口径表现情况看，出口高增与新兴产业快速发展继续对工业生产形成有
力支撑，10 月规模以上工业增加值同比增长 4.8%，高技术制造业、装备
制造业增加值分别增长 17.6%和 10.6%；但内需端进一步走弱，社零受
假期错位影响增速再度放缓，10 月同比增长 0.4%、较上月回落 0.7 个百
分点，1—10 月固定资产投资同比下降 8.1%，房地产开发投资下降
21.6%，降幅较上月分别扩大 0.9和 1.0个百分点。

 往后看，伴随各项稳增长政策落地，包括新型政策性金融工具、专项债
等资金加快形成实物工作量，以及房地产因城施策与城市更新持续推进
等，后期国内经济增长动能有望边际改善。但政策力度与节奏、外需持
续性及海外货币政策变化仍是主要不确定性来源。
 风险提示：1、政策进度不及预期；2、出口回落超预期；3、美联储政策
紧缩超预期。
2026 年 11 月 18 日

### Page 3

1、外需与新动能继续对工业生产形成支撑
10 月份，全国规模以上工业增加值同比增长
4.8%，增速较上月的 5.5%回落 0.7 个百分点，低于
市场一致预期的增长5.3%，1—10月累计增长5.3%。
10 月外需与新动能继续对工业生产形成支撑。
以美元计当月国内出口同比增长35.2%，受此带动规
模以上工业企业出口交货值同比名义增长18.6%，外
需对工业生产的拉动作用持续显现。另一方面，10
月份高技术制造业增加值同比增长17.6%，装备制造
业增长 10.6%，分别快于全部规模以上工业 12.8 和
5.8个百分点；3D打印设备、锂离子电池、工业机器
人产量同比分别增长85.9%、30.0%和26.0%，新能源
汽车产量增长17.1%，新动能产业继续为工业生产注
入强劲动能。
图表 1 规模以上工业增加值增速（%） 图表 2 规上工业企业出口交货值增速（%）
数据来源：同花顺 iFinD、银泰证券研究所

### Page 4

请务必阅读正文之后的免责条款部分 研究专业创新 如意投资随心
分三大门类看，10 月采矿业增加值同比增长
0.7%，制造业增长 5.4%，电力、热力、燃气及水生
产和供应业增长4.4%，较上月分别放缓0.6、0.6和
1.2个百分点。
分行业看，10 月份 41 个大类行业中有 28 个行
业增加值保持同比增长，增长面为68.3%。其中装备
与电子相关行业景气度整体领先，计算机、通信和其
他电子设备制造业增长19.4%，专用设备制造业增长
11.5%，铁路、船舶、航空航天和其他运输设备制造
业增长9.2%，通用设备制造业增长8.5%，电气机械
和器材制造业增长8.1%，汽车制造业增长 0.9%。传
统周期行业多数继续承压，有色金属冶炼和压延加工
业下降4.4%，煤炭开采和洗选业下降2.9%，非金属
矿物制品业下降 2.3%，黑色金属冶炼和压延加工业
下降1.7%，化学原料和化学制品制造业仅增长1.3%。
图表 3 工业三大门类增速（%） 图表 4 10月主要行业增加值同比（%）
数据来源：同花顺 iFinD、银泰证券研究所

2、假期错位影响社零增速再度放缓
10月份，社会消费品零售总额同比增长0.4%，
增速较上月的1.1%回落0.7个百分点，低于市场一
致预期的0.8%，1—10月累计增长1.0%。当月社零
增速放缓，中秋假期错位带来明显扰动，但国内消费
需求偏弱同样是重要原因。
按消费类型分，10月商品零售额同比增长0.3%
（上月为+1.0%），餐饮增长0.7%（上月为+2.1%），
增速分别放缓0.7 和1.4 个百分点。10月份限额以
上单位消费品零售额同比下降4.9%，降幅较上月扩
大2.8个百分点，自4 月以来持续处于负增长区间。
主要商品类别中，汽车类同比下降21.4%（上月为
-21.2%），继续对社零整体形成拖累；此外通讯器材
类同比增长6.5%（上月为31.1%），增速较上月明显
放缓，家用电器和音像器材类增长2.5%（上月为
1.9%）边际有所改善。其它商品类别中，烟酒类增长
2.5%（上月为13.8%），饮料类增长0.3%（上月为

5.0%），文化办公用品类增长4.1%（上月为7.9%），
中西药品类增长3.7%（上月为5.0%），化妆品类增
长2.8%（上月为5.4%），增速较上月均有不同程度
的放缓。

零售额增速4.0个百分点，通讯信息服务类、旅游咨
询租赁服务类、文体休闲服务类零售额较快增长

3、固投延续调整，新动能投资较快增长
1—10月份，全国固定资产投资（不含农户）同
比下降 8.1%，降幅较 1—9 月的-7.2%扩大 0.9个百分

### Page 7

请务必阅读正文之后的免责条款部分 研究专业创新 如意投资随心
点，低于市场一致预期的-6.8。民间固定资产投资同
比下降 11.4%，降幅继续扩大。
分领域看，基础设施投资同比下降 5.1%，降幅
较 1—9月扩大 0.9个百分点；制造业投资下降 2.9%，
降幅扩大 0.8个百分点。
结构上看，投资向新动能集中的特征进一步强
化。1—10 月知识产权产品投资同比增长 9.2%，占
全部投资比重 16.2%，拉动全部投资增长 1.3个百分
点；设备工器具购置投资增长 8.9%，占比 20.3%、
比上年同期提高 3.2个百分点，拉动全部投资增长 1.5
个百分点。高技术产业投资同比增长 4.0%，其中信
息服务业投资增长 20.5%、航空、航天器及设备制造
业增长 10.2%、电子及通信设备制造业增长 6.6%。
基建投资落地进程偏慢，1—10月电力、热力、
燃气及水生产和供应业投资下降 7.7%（1—9 月为
-6.8%），交通运输、仓储和邮政业增长 0.7%（1—9
月为+1.9%），水利、环境和公共设施管理业下降

14.4%（1—9月为-12.8%），投资增速较上月均有不
同程度的放缓。

4、房地产延续筑底，开发投资降幅扩大
1—10 月份，全国房地产开发投资同比下降
21.6%，降幅较1—9 月的-20.6%扩大1.0个百分点。
房屋施工面积下降13.0%，新开工面积下降24.4%，
竣工面积下降 26.6%，开发端各环节仍全面处于低

### Page 9

请务必阅读正文之后的免责条款部分 研究专业创新 如意投资随心
位。
销售端看，1—10 月新建商品房销售面积同比
下降13.7%，降幅较1—9月扩大 0.5个百分点；销
售额下降14.3%，降幅扩大0.4个百分点。
相对积极的是，二手房交易持续改善。1—10
月全国二手房交易网签面积 67971 万平方米、同比
增长11.4%，增速较1—9月加快 0.7个百分点。
房价方面，10月份一线城市新建商品住宅销售
价格环比由上月上涨0.1%转为持平，其中上海、深
圳分别上涨0.2%和0.1%，广州持平、北京下降0.1%；
二线城市环比由持平转为下降0.1%，三线城市下降
0.2%、降幅与上月相同；70个大中城市中新建商品
住宅价格环比上涨城市14个，比上月减少3 个。
二手住宅方面，一线城市环比上涨0.2%、涨幅
与上月持平（上海+0.5%、广州+0.2%、深圳+0.1%、
北京持平），二线、三线城市均下降0.3%；二手住
宅价格环比上涨城市7 个，比上月减少2 个。
同比看，一二三线城市新建商品住宅价格降幅

分别收窄0.4、0.4和0.3个百分点，二手住宅价格
降幅分别收窄1.1、0.4和0.4个百分点，一线城市
价格企稳趋势继续得到确认，但全国层面地产仍处
于筑底阶段。

115年 9月全國賦稅收入初步統計 
一、全國賦稅收入 
（一）本月數 
9 月實徵淨額 4,965 億元，較上年同月增加 813 億元(+19.6%)。 
（二）累計數 
累計 1 至 9 月實徵淨額 3 兆 5,714 億元，較上年同期增加 7,004 億
元(+24.4%)。 
（三）達成率 
1.中央政府總預算業於 9 月 18 日公布，稅課收入較原預算案提高
6,000 億元，併計地方政府及特種基金後，全國稅課收入較預算案
提高 5,852 億元，本次發布均改依法定數計算預算達成率(實徵淨
額占全年預算數)。 
2.累計 1 至 9 月實徵淨額占累計分配預算數 115.0%，占全年預算數
78.8%。 
圖 1 全國賦稅收入及年增率

說明：115 年 5-7 月年增率跳動較大，係因 114 年所得稅結算申報及繳納期限延長至 6 月底所致。

二、主要稅目別變動
（一）9 月實徵淨額與上年同月比較，以營業稅增加 297 億元、營利事業
所得稅增加 254 億元及證券交易稅增加 222 億元較多。
（二）累計 1 至 9 月實徵淨額與上年同期比較，以證券交易稅增加 3,077
億元、營利事業所得稅增加 1,853 億元及營業稅增加 1,203 億元較多。
圖 2 主要稅目別變化
(115 年 1-9 月)
1.營利事業所得稅
(1)9 月實徵淨額 2,007 億元，較上年同月增加 254 億元(+14.5%)，主
因暫繳稅款成長。
(2)累計 1 至 9 月實徵淨額 1 兆 28 億元，較上年同期增加 1,853 億元
(+22.7%)，主因結算申報自繳、暫繳稅款俱增及延分期稅款陸續
入帳。
2.綜合所得稅
9 月實徵淨額 490 億元，較上年同月增加 34 億元(+7.4%)，主因扣繳
稅款增加所致；累計 1 至 9 月實徵淨額 8,279 億元，較上年同期增加
790 億元(+10.6%)，主因結算申報自繳增加，以及盈餘分配、薪資所

得等扣繳稅款均呈成長。
3.營業稅
9月實徵淨額1,414億元，較上年同月增加297億元(+26.6%)，累計1至
9月實徵淨額6,230億元，較上年同期增加1,203億元(+23.9%)，均因內
需穩定，加以電子零組件、資通與視聽產品等進口代徵稅額增加，
惟出口暢旺，適用外銷零稅率營業人申請退稅亦增，抵銷部分增幅。

4.貨物稅
(1)9月實徵淨額100億元，較上年同月減少11億元(-10.0%)，主因汽柴
油減徵幅度擴大，惟電動車大量到港推升進口車稅額，抵銷部分
減幅。
(2)累計1至9月實徵淨額874億元，較上年同期減少207億元(-19.2%)，
除汽柴油降稅因素外，亦與新購汽機車及電動車之減免徵退稅增
加有關。
5.證券交易稅
(1)9 月及累計 1 至 9 月上市上櫃股票平均每日成交值為 1 兆 317 億
元、1 兆 1,461 億元，分別較上年同期(6,006 億元、4,451 億元)增
71.8%、1.6 倍。
(2)9 月 證 交 稅 實 徵 淨 額 534 億 元 ， 較 上 年 同 月 增 加 222 億 元
(+71.2%)；累計 1 至 9 月實徵淨額 5,060 億元，較上年同期增加
3,077 億元(+1.6 倍)。
6.關稅
9 月實徵淨額 157 億元，較上年同月增加 3 億元(+1.9%)，累計 1 至 9
月實徵淨額 1,224 億元，較上年同期增加 80 億元(+7.0%)，均因機械

及電機設備、基本金屬及其製品等進口稅額增加。
7.贈與稅
9 月實徵淨額 23 億元，較上年同月增加 6 億元(+35.7%)，累計 1 至 9
月實徵淨額 211 億元，較上年同期增加 43 億元(+25.3%)，主因大額
稅款案件增加。
8.土地增值稅
9 月實徵淨額 56 億元，較上年同月增加 12 億元(+26.6%)，主因大額
稅款案件增加；累計 1 至 9 月實徵淨額 504 億元，較上年同期減少
14 億元(-2.8%)，主因房地交易量及大額稅款案件減少。
9.印花稅
9 月實徵淨額 31 億元，較上年同月增加 4 億元(+16.2%)，累計 1 至 9
月實徵淨額 175 億元，較上年同期增加 15 億元(+9.0%)，主因銀行放
款稅額成長。
（三）前 9月主要稅目實徵淨額占分配預算數之比率：
1.大於 100%者有證交稅(272%)、遺產稅(155%)、贈與稅(132%)、印花
稅(130%)、營業稅(116%)、關稅(114%)、契稅(111%)、房屋稅(106%)、

綜所稅(101%)、牌照稅(101%)及營所稅(101%)等。
2.低於 100%者有貨物稅(98%)、菸酒稅(97%)、土增稅(85%)、地價稅
(84%)及特銷稅(57%)等。

### Page 1

Asia Vital Components

Solid 3Q26 results; positive tone to 4Q26/ 
2027; reiterate Buy; trim PO to NT$3,650

Reiterate Rating: BUY | PO: 3,650 TWD | Price: 2,790 TWD

3Q26: record-high GM/OPM for five straight quarters 
Asia Vital Components (AVC)’s 3Q26 GM/OPM of 33.9%/28.5% hit a record high for the 
fifth straight quarter, exceeding BofAe of 33.0%/27.5% and consensus of 33.3%/27.9%. 
OP (+25% QoQ, +108% YoY) was 7%/4% above BofAe/consensus, with higher sales 
(+20% QoQ, +52% YoY; 3%/2% above BofAe/consensus). We attribute the results beat 
to solid product mix (server and networking comprised 66% of 3Q26 sales, same as 1Q-
2Q26) and improving production efficiency. Despite a non-operating loss (mainly FX 
loss), NP was 10%/3% above consensus (Exhibit 3).  
Positive tone to 4Q26/2027; ongoing capacity expansion 
We expect 4Q26 sales to rise 19% QoQ and 2027 sales to grow 40% YoY, with mgmt.’s 
still positive tone to 4Q26/2027, mainly driven by AI liquid cooling projects. AVC 
indicated full support to clients’ platform upgrade and shipment pipeline. It should be 
able to pass through the higher raw material cost. In 2027, mgmt. targets ASIC sales mix 
to surpass GPU. AVC also sees solid sales momentum from chassis/rack and CDU, acting 
as the solution provider. Mgmt. maintained 2026 capex guidance at NT$15bn and guided 
NT$15-20bn for 2027 (BofAe unchanged at NT$17bn), mainly for capacity expansion in 
Vietnam. Mgmt. now targets another 50% cold plate capacity expansion in 2027. 
Trim PO to NT$3,650 for a lower P/E set-up; reiterate Buy 
We lift 2026-28E EPS by 3-10%, mainly to reflect the 3Q26 results beat and higher 
margins, driven by ongoing server/networking project ramp-up. Yet, we cut our PO to 
NT$3,650 (23x 2027E EPS), with a lower P/E multiple (2SD above average since 2020; 
Exhibit 5) from NT$3,888 (was 27x; 3SD above) on AI supply chain’s potentially higher 
shipment/pipeline uncertainty due to component shortage. Nevertheless, we reiterate 
Buy on AVC as we believe it should remain one of the key beneficiaries from rising 
liquid-cooling adoption and ongoing GPU/ASIC platform/spec upgrade. We also highlight 
solid sales/margin growth under broad product base and diversified project/client base.

Key Metrics

Company Sector 
IT Hardware

Company Description 
AVC is the leading system-level thermal/cooling component 
maker globally. Key end markets include server, PC, 
smartphone. Key end clients include global CSPs, PC brands, 
smartphone brands. AVC was founded in 1991 and went 
publicly listed on Taiwan Stock Exchange on 27 Sep 2002. It 
is headquartered in Taiwan. Its main production sites are 
located in China (Shenzhen, Wuhan, Shanghai, Dongguan, 
Chengdu), with ongoing capacity expansion in Vietnam.

Investment Rationale 
We assign a Buy rating to AVC given its leading position in 
thermal/cooling component and total solution strategy to 
ride on strong AI market demand, double-digit sales/EPS 
CAGR in 2026-28 with rising liquid cooling sales mix and 
ASP/margin increase.

### Page 6

Price objective basis & risk 
Asia Vital Components (AVMPF) 
Our PO of NT$3,650 is based on 23x 2027E EPS. We use 23x P/E multiple (2SD above 
P/E average since 2020) in view of AVC's double-digit sales/EPS CAGR in 2026-28E. We 
believe AVC's leading position in thermal/cooling component market and strong AI 
market demand will drive rising liquid cooling sales mix and ASP/margin increase.

Upside risks to our PO are: (1) stronger demand in AI server, server, PC, smartphone, (2) 
better operating leverage with larger revenue scale, (3) less intensive competition, (4) 
faster product certification, (5) faster capacity expansion, (6) favorable FX movement 
(USD/RMB and/or USD/NTD) to help on GM and/or non-operating items.

Downside risks to our PO are: (1) softer AI server demand due to GPU/component 
shortage, (2) weaker server/PC/smartphone market demand amid inflation/macro 
uncertainties, (3) GM pressure due to higher material/labor/utility cost, (4) more 
intensive competition, (5) delayed product certification, (6) slower capacity expansion, 
(7) unfavorable FX movement (USD/RMB and/or USD/NTD) to weigh on GM and/or non-
operating items.

FUNDAMENTAL EQUITY OPINION KEY: Opinions include a Volatility Risk Rating, an Investment Rating and an Income Rating. VOLATILITY RISK RATINGS, indicators 
of potential price fluctuation, are: A - Low, B - Medium and C - High. INVESTMENT RATINGS reflect the analyst’s assessment of both a stock’s absolute total return 
potential as well as its attractiveness for investment relative to other stocks within its Coverage Cluster (defined below). Our investment ratings are: 1 - Buy stocks 
are expected to have a total return of at least 10% and are the most attractive stocks in the coverage cluster; 2 - Neutral stocks are expected to remain flat or 
increase in value and are less attractive than Buy rated stocks and 3 - Underperform stocks are the least attractive stocks in a coverage cluster. An investment 
rating of 6 (No Rating) indicates that a stock is no longer trading on the basis of fundamentals. Analysts assign investment ratings considering, among other things, 
the 0-12 month total return expectation for a stock and the firm’s guidelines for ratings dispersions (shown in the table below). The current price objective for a 
stock should be referenced to better understand the total return expectation at any given time. The price objective reflects the analyst’s view of the potential price 
appreciation (depreciation).

Earnings revision: 
We revise up our 2026 earnings estimates by 6% to factor in 3Q26 results (we revise 
down our 2026E revenue estimates by 1%). We revise up 2028E earnings estimates by 
1% to reflect a better product mix with higher contribution from the server business. We 
now expect the GM expansion to be faster due to the higher server business 
contribution, and thus revise up GPM by 1.2/0.3/0.4ppt in 2026/27/28E.

Valuation: 
We reiterate our Buy rating on AVC with a new 12m TP of NT$4,820 (from NT$4,736), 
based on 30.6x 2027E P/E (which is above AVC’s historical average +1 STDV. forward 
trading P/E, reflecting our positive view on AVC on the rising penetration rate of 
liquid-cooled AI servers), and factor in our latest earnings revision. 
Investment thesis and Valuation & Risks

AVC is a global leading supplier of thermal solutions on Server, PC, Telecom and 
consumer electronics. We expect the company’s cooling revenue contributions to grow 
to 66%/71%/74% in 2026/27/28E, driven by: (1) rising computing power of AI servers 
driving liquid cooling demand, (2) capacity ramp-up in Vietnam of 3D Vapor Chambers 
for GPUs and liquid cooling components, and (3) AI PC’s launch in 2025, featuring more 
powerful CPU/ NPU/ Memory. We are Buy rated. Our target price is based on 2027E P/E 
of 30.6x which is higher than its past 5-yr avg.+1stv. of 11x, reflecting the product mix 
upgrade toward servers cooling / liquid cooling with stronger earnings growth and 
better margins, which we believe supports the higher valuation. 
We are Buy rated on AVC with a 12m TP of NT$4,820, based on 30.6 2027E P/E, which is

Ratings, coverage universe and related definitions
Buy (B), Neutral (N), Sell (S) Analysts recommend stocks as Buys or Sells for inclusion on various regional Investment Lists. Being assigned a Buy or Sell
on an Investment List is determined by a stock’s total return potential relative to its coverage universe. Any stock not assigned as a Buy or a Sell on an
Investment List with an active rating (i.e., a  stock that is not Rating Suspended, Not Rated, Early-Stage Biotech, Coverage Suspended or Not Covered), is

deemed Neutral. Each region manages Regional Conviction Lists, which are selected from Buy rated stocks on the respective region’s Investment Lists
and represent investment recommendations focused on the size of the total return potential and/or the likelihood of the realization of the return across
their respective areas of coverage.  The addition or removal of stocks from such Conviction Lists are managed by the Investment Review Committee or
other designated committee in each respective region and do not represent a change in the analysts’ investment rating for such stocks.

Total return potential represents the upside or downside differential between the current share price and the price target, including all paid or
anticipated dividends, expected during the time horizon associated with the price target. Price targets are required for all covered stocks. The total
return potential, price target and associated time horizon are stated in each report adding or reiterating an Investment List membership.
Coverage Universe: A list of all stocks in each coverage universe is available by primary analyst, stock and coverage universe at

https://www.gs.com/research/hedge.html.

### Page 1

기아 (000270)

EARNINGS PREVIEW Company Analysis | 자동차/로봇 | 2026.10.15  
잘 싸웠지만 아쉬운 3분기

3Q26 Preview 
기아는 3Q26E 매출액 31.1조원(+8.2% YoY, -6.0% QoQ), 영업이익 2.2조원
(+53.1% YoY, -14.9% QoQ, OPM 7.2%)을 기록, 컨센서스를 하회할 전망이다. 
물량은 좋았으나, 아쉬운 믹스와 환율 
글로벌 도매 판매 84.7만대(+7.8% 이하 YoY), 중국 제외 판매량은 83.0만대
(+8.6%)를 기록했다. EV·HEV 확대에 따른 ASP 상승은 지속되고 있다. 전쟁으로 인
한 원자재 상승으로 3분기 재료비 부담은 전분기 대비 3,000억~4,000억원 증가할 
것으로 예상된다. EV 판매 비중은 2분기 13%에서 3분기 15%, 유럽은 35%에서 
43%로 확대되며 유럽 내 마진 희석은 불가피하다. 원화 강세에 따른 영업상 부담은 
판매보증충당부채 환입 등으로 상쇄돼 3분기 환율 영향은 제한적일 전망이다. 판매보
증충당부채 환입은 QoQ +5,000억원 수준으로 추정된다. 4분기부터 환율 효과는 부정
적일 것이며, 환율 10원 변동 시 연간 손익 민감도는 약 1,500억원 수준이다. 
투자의견은 Buy를 유지하고 목표주가는 165,000원으로 하향 
미국 조지아 공장의 텔루라이드 HEV(2.8만대)와 HMGMA의 스포티지 HEV(1.0만대) 
신규 생산으로 북미의 견조한 HEV 수요에 대응하며, 2027년 믹스 개선이 기대된다. 
유럽은 EV3·EV5로 점유율을 확대하는 가운데 유럽 내 할인율 하락과 EV 잔존가치 
회복으로 판매 여건이 개선되고 있다. 향후 IAA 규제에 따른 법인·플릿 혜택 제한은 
중국 업체의 원가 우위를 줄일 수 있을 전망이다. 
원화 약세 지속을 반영해 이익 추정치를 하향하며, 이에 따라 목표주가를 하향한다.  
다만, 현재 주가는 12M Fwd P/E 기준 4.8배로 Legacy OEM 평균(7.7배)보다 낮은 
수준, 저가 매수 기회라 판단된다. 현재 배당 수익률은 6% 수준으로, 상대적으로 높은 
배당 매력은 주가 하방을 지지할 전망이다. 
 Analyst 이병근 
bglee@ls-sec.co.kr

 절대 (달러환산) 6.1  13.8  (8.6)

3Q26Re: 신규 시설 매출 인식 개시 
연결 기준 매출액 1조 4,903억원(+18.5%YoY, 이하 YoY 생략), 영업이익 6,401억원
(+1.0%, OPM 42.9%)로 컨센서스를 상회하는 실적 기록. 1~4공장이 완전 가동을 유지하는 
한편, 5공장은 PPQ 배치 생산 본격화되면서 매출 기여 확대. 상업 생산 전환이 예상되는 
2H27부터 이익 기여 커질 것으로 기대. 
연초 인수한 Rockville 공장은 3Q에도 안정적인 상업 생산을 이어갔으며 2Q 생산 물량의 
매출이 인식되며 매출 성장에 기여. 동사는 Rockville 공장 매출 인식 등을 반영하여 기존 
+15~20% 성장에서 +20%로 연간 가이던스 상향.   
고객사 수주 회복 기대 
3Q26 기준 누적 수주액은 219억 달러로 전분기 대비 소폭 증가했으나 과거 연간 수주액 
증가 대비 부족한 수준. 저조한 수주 실적은 지난해 초 의약품에 대한 관세 불확실성 증가로 
고객사들의 수주 문의가 저조했던 영향. 26년 초부터 관세 불확실성이 낮아지며 수주 문의
는 회복 추세. 약 1년 정도 논의 기간이 소요되는 것을 고려할 때 26년말~27년 초 수주 회
복 확인 가능할 것으로 기대. 
안정적인 공급망 유지가 중요한 CDMO 산업 특성상 노조 파업 가능성도 고객사 수주를 늦
추는 요인으로 판단. 다만, 10/16 인천 고등법원이 쟁의행위 가처분 항고심에 대해 인용 결
정을 하며 기존 3개 공정에서 9개 공정으로 확대되며 파업 우려는 다소 완화. 
투자 의견 Buy, 목표 주가 200만원 유지 
매수 의견과 목표 주가 200만원을 유지. 원달러 약세에도 불구하고 5공장 Ramp-up과 
Rockville 공장 매출 기여로 견조한 실적 성장 지속될 것으로 예상. 파업 이슈 해소와 대형 
수주 계약이 주가 상승을 이끌 것으로 판단.

매출액 5,754  30.2  -9.1  6,018  -4.4  
영업이익 899  61.4  -13.5  979  -8.2  
세전계속사업이익 268  -53.0  -87.9  1,001  -73.2

영업이익률 (%) 15.6  +3.0 %pt -0.8 %pt 16.3  -0.7 %pt 
지배순이익률 (%) -2.0  적전 적전 12.5  -14.5 %pt 
자료: 유안타증권

Company Report  2026.11.12

상선, 방산, 해양, 엔진 모든 사업 영위하는 전천후 기업 
경쟁사 대비 1) 가장 넓은 사업 영역을 보유, 2) 시가총액 또한 높음. 이러한 특성은 동사의 
사업 포트폴리오 균형 관점에서 장점이나, 반대로 투자 매력의 집중을 일정 부분 희석. 경쟁
사 대비 주가 상승 트리거가 가장 폭넓게 존재하나, 상승 탄력은 그만큼 제한. 
상선: FY27F 매출인식은 FY23-25 수주 물량, 가스선 매출비중 하락 
FY27F 상선부문 매출은 FY23-25 수주 물량이 인식되며 (일부 FY26F 수주), FY25 수주
한 물량이 연간 40% 수준의 가장 높은 매출기여도를 보일 것. 2H27F부터 올해 수주한 일
부 선박의 매출인식 또한 발생하며, 연중 가스선 매출 비중이 하락하고 컨테이너선 매출 비
중이 상승. 
방산: 좋게 보자면 불안하고, 나쁘게 보자면 아쉬운 
MASGA를 국내 야드에서 진행한다는 대전제로 경쟁사와 같은 적정가치를 부여해 왔으나, 
미국 현지 건조와 같은 여러 가능성을 고려하면 다음과 같은 불확실성 존재: 1) 중간지주사
와의 미국내 JV 설립 관련 투자 지분 미정, 2) 신규 조선소 건립시 미국내 수주와 매출까지 
걸리는 시간. 
투자의견 BUY 유지, 다 깎아서 봐도 충분한 상승여력 
상선부문 26.5조, 방산 8조, 선박엔진 9조, 발전엔진 (FY30F 기준) 13.5조 수준의 SOTP 
밸류에이션 기반하여 목표주가 643,000원 제시. 발전엔진 증설로 인한 미래가치를 반영하
나, 상선부문 Target P/O multiple 하향 (0.7x, 업종 공통)과 MASGA 적정가치 하향으로 
목표주가 자체는 하락. 보수적 멀티플 제시함에도 주가 상승 여력은 충분히 확보된 상황. 업
종 전체적 긍정요인 외의 동사 주가 상승 트리거는 1) 미국 현지 조선소 투자주체 발탁, 2) 
DC향 발전엔진 추가 수주, 3) MASGA 국내 건조 물량 수주.

Euro Area: Industrial production, ZEW survey of expectations, consumer confidence.
United Kingdom: Unemployment rate, CPI, consumer sentiment, retail sales.

United States: FOMC minutes, regional manufacturing surveys, housing market data, retail sales.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
ECONOMIC INSIGHT
It was already our view that the RBA was likely done hiking
the cash rate after the November increase. Since then, our
conviction on that call has strengthened. Later today we
will publish the November Market Outlook with revised
forecasts. GDP growth, the labour market and housing

market have all been revised down in 2027.
Underlying inflation has been revised down slightly in the
second half of 2027, bringing the year-ended figure for
calendar 2027 more decisively inside the RBA’s 2–3% target
range than our previous forecast round. The unemployment
rate forecast has been revised up to peak above 5%. Based on
this revised forecast profile, an increase in the cash rate from
here would be unlikely and – we would argue – undesirable.

A range of considerations underlie this forecast revision. The
housing market has undershot our forecasts over the past
couple of months. Turnover and financing activity have been
coming in under our earlier expectations. Mortgage loan
inquiries have taken another leg down in October after the
September rate hike, after finding something of a level in prior
months, with the additional impact of the November rate hike
still to come.
We are also seeing early signs of a turn in the consumer.

Consumer income and spending have so far been quite
resilient to pressures from fuel prices and rising interest
rates. But our Westpac Card Tracker is starting to show that
discretionary services spending is turning down again in Q4,
joining the discretionary goods category that was already quite
weak. We would not over-emphasise this point, but it does
imply that the risks of further surprising resilience are fading.
Subsequent to the November Monetary Policy Board (MPB)

meeting, business survey data has shown weaker conditions
and a sharp decline in forward orders. They also show
further evidence that current inflation is mostly a non-
labour cost shock that is not being fully passed through to
consumer prices.
As we noted following the November MPB meeting, oil
and fuel prices are already materially lower than the track
assumed in the RBA’s forecasts. Dated Brent is nearly one-
fourth lower than on 28 October, the date the RBA’s forecasts

were finalised, and spot Brent is around 15% lower. This
significantly lowers the risk of a new wave of pass-through of
fuel costs to other prices, as well as easing consumer inflation
perceptions and expectations.
An RBA peak of greater conviction
Forecast changes
• A full update to our forecasts has been published in our
November Market Outlook.
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
In the media conference following the November meeting,
Governor Bullock flagged that it was a good time to “take

stock” of the policy tightening to date. Both we and the
market read this as a signal that the MPB would pause at the
December meeting. The 6:3 split vote at the meeting also
suggests that some external members are more attentive to
downside risks than the internal members.
A rate hike in February 2027 cannot be ruled out but it is
looking even less likely given signs in the data and surveys.
The MPB will at least discuss a hike in February. The staff and

senior officials remain sensitive to inflation risks and have a
downbeat view of supply capacity growth.
Given what we are seeing in the economy now, though,
a rate hike in 2027 would require either a material new
inflation shock that has not yet occurred, or would
constitute a policy error on the part of the RBA (something
that we will never forecast as a base case, knowing the talents
and capabilities of the staff and the Board). We concur with

the dissenting voters in the MPB that the downside risks need
more attention.
Policy is clearly restrictive, and the risk of overdoing the
inflation correction is growing. And by the time we get to
February, we think there will be more evidence that the
domestic economy is slowing materially, and the inflation
outlook with it.
“A deeper housing downturn and early signs of
cracks in the consumer sector point to an even
softer 2027."

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: cost of living front and centre
the labour market while showing significant concern over
the strength and persistence of inflation, focusing on the
passthrough of energy and technology input inflation. His
conclusion for policy was clear: "if, as I expect, the monthly

rate of inflation improves in October, I am leaning toward
supporting an increase in our policy rate of 25 basis points
at the FOMC's December meeting"; but "if the October CPI
inflation data out later today are similar to the past two
monthly readings—with core CPI inflation around 0.3 percent
or even higher—then a stronger policy move may be needed".
Regional Fed Presidents Goolsbee, Logan and Hammack also
kept the focus on inflation risks and stated or implied the FOMC

needs to act. Market participants have responded to this week’s
updates, pricing a 25bp hike in for the December and January
meetings, and a cumulative 3.5 hikes by September. We believe
this pricing overstates the need for policy tightening given core
inflation ex shelter remains relatively benign at 2.4% on a six-
month annualised basis and the labour market is balanced; but
it will take time for price concerns to subside.
While we agree that the US economy is strong in aggregate,

US households are under pressure from the cost of living, and
their investment decision making is constrained by borrowing
costs. Highlighting this, US existing home sales fell 5.5% in
October after a downwardly revised 1.0% gain in September,
leaving the level of sales below the cycle lows of 2009 and
2010. Our November Market Outlook provide a full update on
our expectations for the US and the world.
In Australia, the Westpac-MI Consumer Sentiment Index

fell 2.7% to 78.2 in November, its weakest reading since
late-2022 and a level historically associated with periods of
severe economic stress. Renewed cost-of-living and interest
rate pressures were front and centre following another lift
in inflation, including sharply higher petrol prices, and the
RBA’s November rate increase. Mortgage holders are feeling
the squeeze most acutely, with sentiment across this group
falling to a record low. Consumers have become noticeably

more downbeat on the near-term economic outlook – the
relevant sub-index dropping 10.2% to be 22% below its
long-run average. Job-loss fears also continue to edge up.
Businesses are similarly troubled. The latest NAB business
survey showed confidence slipped further to –15 in October as
conditions held near their weakest level since the pandemic.
Of concern for the outlook, forward orders fell to –12, also
the weakest read since the pandemic, and capital expenditure

intentions deteriorated. Purchase costs continue to grow
materially faster than selling prices, further compressing
already tight margins.
Housing provides a clear example of how caution and tighter
financial conditions can jolt activity. The value of new dwelling
finance approvals plunged 15% in Q3, the largest quarterly
decline in the series’ history, taking approvals 25% lower year-
to-date. Investor lending bore the brunt of the adjustment,
falling 30% over the quarter as higher rates combined

with the housing tax changes announced in May. Owner-
occupier lending also weakened, however, with first-home
buyers proving particularly sensitive to the deterioration in
affordability and market conditions.
Taken together, the data points to a further loss of momentum
across the domestic economy, consistent with Westpac’s
expectation for subdued growth and concern over downside
activity risks.
Meanwhile, in the US, October’s headline CPI met our

expectations but at 0.4% was a touch above the consensus
view. Energy and food both contributed meaningfully to the
result, but core inflation was also marginally stronger than
forecast, +0.3%. Core goods inflation has accelerated over the
past four months to average a gain of 0.2% as businesses look
to pass tariffs, tech and energy input cost inflation through.
Shelter meanwhile continued to rise at an above-average pace,
gaining 0.3% in October.

The market impact of the October CPI report was accentuated
by hawkish remarks from Fed Governor Waller prior to the
release. He expressed confidence in economic activity and
Elliot Clarke, Head of International Economics
Ryan Wells, Economist
Illiana Jain, Economist

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
Post election, where are we left?
This week markets and the economy needed to digest
the General Election results, as well as data on how the
economy is tracking through the final months of the year. A
centre-right coalition looks more likely to ultimately prevail,

although it may be a while before we know for sure and
longer again before a stable government emerges. Watch
this space as paths to victory for the centre-left remain.
Economic data are consistent with a still-strong external
sector but a weaker domestically-generated growth
impulse. Our upcoming Economic Overview next week will
examine these trends in more depth.
The real star of the show this week was the outcome of the
2026 General Election, which delivered an election night lead

to the centre-right coalition of the National, ACT and NZ First
parties. The preliminary result was a 67-57 seat margin in an
enlarged 131 seat parliament. Hence the centre-right won on
election night with a slender one-seat margin, given that 66
seats are required to form a majority. Financial markets and
the business sector generally cheered this outcome given their
preference for a centre-right government. However, the margins
are tight and there is the potential for the result to switch around

when the final vote count becomes available on November 27.
National leads Labour by narrow margins in two electorates
(Wairarapa and Hamilton East) and could lose those seats
when all is said and done. Also, special votes tend to swing
more to the left than the right historically and hence could
boost the centre-left bloc. The star performer of the election
was the Opportunity Party which debuted in parliament with
7 seats. The centre-left would require their support to get

over the line in all scenarios, whereas the centre-right might or
might not need them depending on how the special votes and
final electorate results play out. There seems a clearer path to
victory for the centre-right – especially if they can come to an
agreement with Opportunity – but clients should be wary of
complacency (which we note may be a risk).
A further complication is that the election left the incumbent
National Party in a much-weakened position as their coalition

partners picked up seats at their expense, and because their
much-reduced share of the party vote (29.2%) meant that
no National Party list MPs made it into parliament. Several
seasoned and senior government ministers needed a list seat
to make it into the new government including Finance Minister
Nicola Willis. Even if the final results confirm a centre-right
coalition win, there will be fierce debate within the coalition
on which party staffs key ministerial roles. ACT party leader

David Seymour is making a strong play for Finance Minister,
which Prime Minister Christopher Luxon is fiercely resisting.
There is the potential for tough and protracted coalition
negotiations to play out well into the Christmas period.
Indeed, I spoke on a post-election panel with an ex-National
Party senior minister this week who expressed the view that
it might not be until Waitangi Day (February 6) before a new
government is formed. Should anything like this play out then

the implications for economic and market uncertainty could
be significant. The initial relief rally in the New Zealand dollar
might prove premature in that scenario.
For interest rate markets, the main conclusion drawn is that as
a centre-left government seems a lower probability prospect,
then so also is the likelihood of a return to a ‘dual mandate’
for the RBNZ. Chief Economist Paul Conway made remarks
this week suggesting ongoing concern with inflation risks,

suggesting he is still in the hike camp at this stage. This has
seen markets firm up short-term OCR expectations such that a
25bp OCR increase in December is now seen as high probability
(85-90% currently). Bond markets cheered the prospect of
potentially tighter fiscal policy that could come with a centre-
right government. The spread between the 10-year government
bond and swap spread narrowed 4 basis points over the week
and the 10-year government bond yield differential to the US

fell to -49 basis points – the most negative since 2019.
Economic data showed further evidence of the two-speed
economy that is showing above-trend growth yet at the same
time feels quite unsatisfying to most voters. The low exchange
rate, generally robust global demand and tight supply
conditions in soft commodity markets have been delivering
consistently strong export returns for a couple of years now.
This week we saw another very strong GDT Pulse auction that

propelled whole milk powder futures higher – up some 20-25%
on year ago levels in USD terms. Significant upside risk for our
2026/27 Farmgate Milk Price forecast of $9.75/kgMS now exists,
which we will be delving into more deeply in our Economic
Overview to be released on Wednesday. The manufacturing
sector appears to remain in a solid state, with the manufacturing
PMI slipping a touch in October to 54.9 from 56.3 in September.
Short-term visitor arrivals have resumed their prior strong

uptrend, growing almost 3% for a second consecutive month
in September and almost 10% higher than a year earlier.
While arrivals from key European markets remain well below
year-earlier levels – likely reflecting the disruption to travel
through key Middle Eastern hubs along with higher jet fuel
prices – this has been more than offset by a very sharp lift in
arrivals from China. Indeed, Chinese arrivals in September
were almost two-thirds higher than the same month last year.

Michael Gordon
Senior Economist

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
And in the September quarter, arrivals from China surpassed
their pre-pandemic level for the first time. The number of
Australian citizens visiting New Zealand grew just under 6%yr
in September, but growth momentum from New Zealand’s
number one source market (a 30% share) does appear to have

slowed somewhat in recent months.
Less positive trends were evident in the more domestically
orientated areas of the economy. The most concrete indicator
of the NZ economy – concrete poured – dropped back a
touch in the September quarter, while consumer spending
in fell 0.7% in October. We have concerns on the quality
of the consumer spending data given that bank-sourced
data indicates a rise in consumer spending in October. But
nonetheless it’s probably reasonable to infer that higher fuel

costs in October crimped household budgets.
Housing market data for October were also week. House sales
fell and are down almost 22% y/y, while house prices fell in
October for the fifth consecutive month to be down almost
2% y/y. The weak performance of the housing market is likely
another factor weighing on consumer spending and residential
investment. That’s another topic we will be taking a look at in
next week’s Economic Overview.
Net migration flows for September confirm still modest

population growth. Arrivals of non-New Zealand citizens
continues to drift higher while departures declined a touch.
The net outflow of New Zealanders remains relatively high but
is declining, as signs of firmer employment growth emerge in
New Zealand at the same time as the Australian labour market
shows signs of weakening.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUS: Oct Labour Force – Employment Change (000s)
Nov 19, Last: –39.3, Westpac f/c: 30
Market f/c: 27.5, Range: 9.9 to 42
Employment fell by 39.3k in September, well below expectations
for a 20k lift. Some of this weakness may reflect an unwind of
a possible residual seasonality stemming from recent changes

to the survey design, which saw August come in stronger.
Under the monthly noise, the labour market is loosening
gradually as jobs growth runs slower than population growth.
While some leading indicators point to near-term stability
– such as job ads – business surveys and detail within the
employment data point to some downside risk.
For October, we have pencilled in a lift in employment of +30k,
which would see employment growth hold steady on a three-

month average basis.
AUS: Nov Westpac Housing Sentiment (index)
Nov 17, Last: 85.3
The Westpac Housing Sentiment Index that appears in our
regular Housing Pulse reports will now be published on a
monthly basis a week after Westpac-Melbourne Institute
Consumer Sentiment is released. The Index is a composite
based on housing-related sentiment measures from the survey
and has proven to be an extremely useful predictor of turning
points in housing markets, both nationally and across states.

At 85.3, the Oct monthly Index read was holding near historic
lows, well below the long run avg of 100. The Nov update will
capture reactions to the RBA’s latest interest rate rise and the
deepening and broadening housing downturn. The release
will also include results from two new questions added to
the regular survey on consumers’ property buying and selling
intentions over the next twelve months.
AUS: Q3 Wage Price Index (%qtr)
Nov 18, Last: 0.8, Westpac f/c: 1.0

Market f/c: 1.0, Range: 0.9 to 1.1
The Wage Price Index (WPI) rose 0.8% in Q2, leaving annual
growth at 3.2%. Private sector wages continued to decelerate,
in line with a softer labour market, while public sector wages
provided a bit of an offset.
The Fair Work Commission's (FWC) 2026 Annual Wage Review
handed down a larger-than-expected 4.75% increase in award
wages. This will have a noticeable impact on the Q3 WPI, as the
wage increases stemming from the review are typically passed

on during the September quarter.
We have pencilled in a rise of 1.0% for Q3, lifting the annual
pace to 3.4%yr. Detail on wages growth by bargaining stream
will be closely watched for evidence of broader diffusion
beyond arrangements directly linked to the FWC's decision.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUS: Oct Labour Force – Unemployment Rate (%)
Nov 19, Last: 4.7, Westpac f/c: 4.7
Market f/c: 4.7, Range: 4.6 to 4.8
Once again, the surprise in employment coincided with a surprise
in labour force participation, which fell 0.2ppts to 66.8% from
its near cycle high of 67.0% in August. This translated to a 26.2k

decline in the size of the labour force, which was less than the
drop in employment. As a result, the unemployment rate ticked
slightly higher in September, from 4.65% to 4.72% (although it
was steady at 4.7% to one decimal place), a new cycle high.
For October, we have the participation rate steady at 66.8%,
which together with the forecast rise in employment, would
see the unemployment rate also hold steady at 4.7%. Early
signs of rising retrenchments are a key signal to watch over the

coming months, especially if new hiring across other areas of
the economy starts to dry up.
NZ: Oct Selected Consumer Prices
Nov 17
Stats NZ’s monthly Selected Prices provides an update on
around half of the full CPI, including many of the more volatile
items. In terms of the big groups, we’re expecting a 0.1%
rise in overall food prices, with fresh produce prices typically
soft at this time of year. We also expect continued softness
in housing rents. However, the key focus will be on transport

costs. Petrol prices are expected to be up 11%. And we’ll be
watching to see if high global fuel prices are contributing to
further increases in airfares after recent large increases. There
is related uncertainty around holiday accommodation costs
(which drive much of the month-to-month volatility in overall
prices).
NZ: Q4 RBNZ Survey of Expectations (%yr)
Nov 17, Inflation 2yrs Ahead, Last: 2.34
With an easing in headline inflation in the September

quarter, we’re likely to see some easing in near-term inflation
expectations (one year ahead). However, longer term inflation
expectations, particularly the closely-watched two years
ahead measure, are likely to remain broadly steady. While
interest rates are moving higher, businesses are continuing to
report pressures on operating costs. In addition, despite their
recent easing, oil prices remain elevated. The only time longer-
term inflation expectations have moved materially below 2.3%

was in the mid-2010s. That followed an extended period when
inflation was running below 2%.

2 of 8 October 19, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
The U.S. Capex Boom No One is Talking About—And Its Implications
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Ariana Chiu, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
We have long been of the belief that the U.S. is on the cusp of an inbound FDI capex boom
that can result in numerous positive spillover effects for the U.S. economy. The latest

figures from the U.S. Department of Commerce bear this out.
Exhibit 1 highlights the growing push among foreign multinationals to be inside the U.S.,
with FDI totaling nearly $240B in the first six months of this year. That is a staggering
103% jump from a year ago and places FDI inflows on track for a record year.
The latest inbound capex already adds to the massive scale of foreign investment in the
U.S. To wit, the accumulated stock of FDI in the U.S. stands at nearly $6T, based on the

latest figures from the Bureau of Economic Analysis (BEA). Manufacturing accounts for
roughly 43% of the total, and, at last count, some 10,000 foreign affiliates were operating
in the U.S.
These firms are largely invisible to the average U.S. investor, but the activities of U.S.
affiliates of foreign multinationals are substantial and competitive- and growth-enhancing.
To this point and based on the latest data from the BEA, foreign affiliates in the U.S.

employed nearly 8.6 million U.S. workers in 2024. That represents 6.1% of total private
sector employment, with manufacturing and retail the two most prominent sectors
of employment. Roughly 24% of all manufacturing jobs in the U.S. are from foreign
subsidiaries. In terms of pay, U.S. workers at these firms make $89,000 annually, 7% higher
than the national average, according to the Global Business Alliance.
Foreign affiliates also contributed some $1.5T to U.S. GDP; comprised some 15.7% of total

U.S. private sector investment; accounted for 12.4% of total U.S. research & development
(R&D) expenditures; paid roughly 20% of all U.S. federal taxes; and made up 22.5% of
U.S. exports and 26.2% of U.S. imports.1 These are the direct effects of U.S. subsidiaries
of foreign companies operating in the U.S. The indirect effects are even greater in terms
of more income, jobs, growth and tax revenue—all bolstering the long-term competitive
advantage of the U.S. In a nutshell, foreign firms operating in the U.S. are an important

ingredient of U.S. economic growth.
There are multiple factors behind surging inflows: One, due to rising U.S. trade
protectionism, foreign multinationals increasingly want to be “inside” the U.S. economy,
not outside. Companies that once served the U.S. market exclusively through exports
increasingly face higher costs and more uncertainties and hence want to be “inside.”
Two, with a $32T-plus economy with extraordinary consumer purchasing power, foreign

firms prefer to be closer to their customers via investment than just trade (exports). There
is no more dynamic consumer in the world than the American shopper.
Three, America’s massive investment cycle in AI, data centers, semiconductors, power
generation, etc., has created numerous investment opportunities for many foreign firms
that don’t want to miss out on America’s surging capex cycle.
Four, and finally, abundant energy supplies, deep capital markets and a highly developed

technological ecosystem have also induced more FDI inflows.
As more U.S. affiliates of foreign multinationals set up shop in the U.S., think more job
creation across the country, more manufacturing output, more locally sourced purchases,
more U.S. exports, more spending on R&D, and more tax revenue for local communities.
And over the long run, think a more globally competitive U.S. economy.
1 Activities of U.S. Affiliates of Foreign Multinational Enterprises, 2024” and “Activities of U.S. Affiliates of Foreign

Multinational Enterprises, 2023”, BEA July 2026.
Investment Implications
The boom in foreign investment in
the U.S. should continue to support
employment, productivity, corporate
earnings, and broader U.S. economic
competitiveness, in our view. Hence
our U.S. bias.

### Page 3

3 of 8 October 19, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
And speaking of global competitiveness, Exhibit 2A is very illustrative of the magnet pull of
the U.S. economy to attract FDI versus the rest of the world. Of new greenfield investment
this decade, note the overriding preference for the U.S., accounting for nearly 16% of total
global greenfield investment this decade. America’s share is larger than the next three
countries combined—the United Kingdom, India, and Germany. And note that America’s

share of new investment is more than 5X that of China’s. Most of this new investment
has gone into manufacturing sectors like electrical equipment, computers, appliances and
batteries and has originated from Asia and Europe.
The bulk of foreign investment comes from Europe, followed by Japan and Canada, but as
Exhibit 2B highlights, there is a new dynamic playing out when it comes to America’s ability
to attract capital. There is a new source in town: a handful of developing nations (namely

Taiwan, South Korea, Mexico and Brazil). Mexican manufacturers, Taiwan technology
leaders, South Korea chip leaders, Brazil industrial firms, India pharmaceutical firms—these
companies and more are realizing that in today’s trade environment, the best way to serve
the American market is not necessarily through exports but through investment—investing
locally. FDI flows from developing nations reached $63B in the 12 months through June,
more than double the level a year prior ($24.5B), according to the BEA. Fueled by insatiable

AI demand, flows from South Korea alone totaled $23.4B in the first half of 2026. The
upshot is that an increasingly important source of capital for the U.S. is coming from an
unlikely source: the developing nations.
More fire for the capex boom. The coming boom in FDI inflows is supportive of our thesis
that the U.S. stands on the cusp of a major industrial upgrade. We are in the early innings
of a structural—not cyclical—capex cycle that should lead to the rebuilding of domestic

supply chains, higher value-added manufacturing, and more AI-driven productivity growth.
Foreign firms, in our opinion, will be part of this process.
That is important to recognize because remember the following: when a foreign company
builds a factory or an R&D facility or expands its existing manufacturing capacity right
here in the U.S., good things happen. Demand for U.S. goods and services goes up (as does
demand for construction, materials, logistics and professional services). New jobs are created,

boosting incomes of U.S. workers, while supporting more local spending and consumption.
And because many foreign firms often bring advanced manufacturing technologies when
investing in the U.S., the end results include a boost in productivity and innovation in various
sectors of the economy.
All the above, of course, are the secret ingredients to stronger U.S. economic growth, rising
corporate earnings and higher equity prices. With a little help from our friends, America’s

industrial renewal is gaining strength and momentum.

MARKET VIEW
Europe’s Electoral Cycle and Market Risks
Ehiwario Efeyini, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
As midterm elections approach for the U.S. next month, voters across Europe will also
enter their own election season starting this November and continuing throughout
2027. Over the next year, 52% of the total EU population, representing 46% of EU GDP
across eight countries, will go to the polls, including voters in three of its four largest

economies (Exhibit 3). Having announced snap general elections earlier this month,
Spain will vote for members of its lower and upper house on November 29. A series of
parliamentary elections are then due for 2027 in Estonia (by March 7), Finland (on April
18), Greece (by July 25), Slovakia (by September 28), Poland (by November 11) and Italy
(by December 22). And perhaps most consequentially France will stage Presidential
elections on April 18, with a potential second round of voting set for May 2.

For the EU as a whole, the significance of these coming elections will be the potential
for a shift toward more populist, domestically oriented policy priorities from leading
opposition candidates (particularly in France, Spain and Poland) which could obstruct the
process of closer EU integration. In the nearer term, adoption of the new seven-year
(2028-2034) EU budget due by the end of 2027 could be impeded. And over the longer
term, renewed efforts toward an EU-wide capital market union to deepen and attract

more inward investment into European markets could also be further delayed.
At the country level, investors have seen bond yields rise globally in 2026 on the back of
higher inflation and concerns over fiscal sustainability. The debt question has arguably
been most pressing in parts of Europe, especially given the likelihood that upcoming
elections make for domestic increases in government largesse. And of the eight
countries going to the polls over the next year, France remains the biggest source of

concern. Interest rate spreads on French 10-year sovereign bonds have overtaken those
in both Greece and Italy this year (Exhibit 4), driven by investor concerns over public
debt. The 2027 government budget unveiled earlier this month projected a deficit of
5.0% of GDP (well in excess of the 3% specified in the EU Stability and Growth Pact),
but remained insufficiently expansive to nullify the risk that the current government
could be unseated later this year if it is forced through the fragmented parliament. This

comes on top of an existing debt load of close to 120% of GDP and a halving of official
2026 growth forecasts from 1.0% at the start of the year to 0.5% in September. In
short, all four variables in France’s debt sustainability equation currently stand as major
headwinds, and the two leading candidates in the 2027 election have shown limited
appetite for fiscal consolidation over the coming years.

Investment Implications
We remain cautious on European
markets overall, despite seeing
pockets of strength in selected
areas such as defense, data center
infrastructure and renewable energy.
On top of growing uncertainty
from the upcoming electoral cycle

and interest rate outlook, rising
competition and slowing demand
from China continue to challenge
key segments of the Consumer
Discretionary sector. And limited
direct AI concentration also leaves
Europe under-exposed to the key
growth theme that continues to buoy
equity markets in the U.S., Japan and
emerging Asia.

Intervention from the ECB under its 2022 bond purchase program is also unlikely at this

stage given that the spread widening in France has not appeared either disorderly or
unwarranted. Meanwhile, conditions could potentially be aggravated by expected ECB rate
hikes in response to still above-target inflation across the eurozone. Indeed recent moves
to tighten monetary policy from a number of major central banks have been read most
hawkishly in Europe. Most of the increase in European bond yields has come from higher
inflation breakevens as opposed to real yields in the U.S. and Japan. And despite some

paring back of tightening expectations in the wake of recent bond volatility, the market is
still pricing three more policy rate increases over the next 12 months.
Euro depreciation and an emerging shift toward more protectionist trade policy with China
could further complicate the ECB policy outlook. China posted a record $35B trade surplus,
according to Eurostat, with the EU in September (alongside its $938B total surplus for the
first three quarters of 2026), and the two markets have agreed to reduce the EU bilateral

deficit by capping imports of hybrid cars for four years. Hybrids account for less than 2%
of total EU imports from China, but this could potentially mark the start of a broader shift
toward more trade protection in Europe with its largest foreign supplier which risks adding
further upward pressure to cost-push inflation. And though the leading candidates in the
French election may favor resuming oil and gas imports from Russia, Europe also remains

among the most at risk from the global energy shock. Indeed some 56% of the region’s
crude oil consumption is in the form of diesel product according to the International Energy
Agency, more than twice the U.S. share.
The 2- to 10-year eurozone yield curve has already flattened significantly since the start
of the year. And at just over 30 basis points as of mid-October, it remains flatter than
its equivalents in the U.S., UK and Japan. Outside Energy and Technology, Financials have

been among the strongest-performing sectors in Europe this year. But additional ECB
rate hikes could lead to further curve flattening, compressing bank net interest margins
and contributing to slower growth—particularly given that rate increases are coming in
response to supply-driven inflation rather than reflecting stronger economic activity.
We nonetheless continue to see pockets of strength in European markets. Growing
defense budgets should remain a tailwind for Industrials as national governments look to

meet their North Atlantic Treaty Organization targets over the next several years. And the
sector should be further supported by indirect exposure to AI investment through electric
cabling, power infrastructure and building materials for data centers. Leaders in renewable
energy equipment and services are also likely to be beneficiaries of grid upgrades and
broadening electrification globally, particularly given elevated fossil fuel prices. But our view

on the region remains cautious overall. On top of growing uncertainty from the upcoming
electoral cycle and interest rate outlook, rising competition and slowing demand from
China continue to challenge key segments of Europe’s Consumer Discretionary sector.
Limited direct AI concentration in technology hardware, cloud services and application
software also leaves Europe underexposed to the key growth theme that continues to buoy
equity markets in the U.S., Japan and emerging Asia.

### Page 6

6 of 8 October 19, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
Cautious Optimism Entering Fiscal Year 2027
Ariana Chiu, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
“A renewed focus on rebuilding fiscal space is urgent, given elevated debt, higher
borrowing costs, multiplying demands on public budgets and heightened uncertainty.” So
warned the IMF about the global fiscal outlook last week in its updated World Economic

Outlook report. Much is at stake for developed nations in Europe and Asia facing high
existing debt, unfavorable demographics and more modest growth.
For the U.S., between structurally higher mandatory outlays, the administration’s $1.5T
proposed defense package, and the 10-year U.S. Treasury yield sitting above 5%, spending
is likely headed higher. We continue to fade concerns of an impending U.S. debt crisis,
however, largely because economic growth continues to surprise to the upside. Expanding

output is good news for the debt-to-GDP ratio, which remains a touch below 100%.
Indeed, rather than being led higher on account of un-anchored inflation expectations, the
10-year U.S. Treasury yield has repriced to reflect better-than-expected nominal growth
(Exhibit 5A). Higher yields in the face of slowing growth, meanwhile, would be a different
story, not only for the fiscal outlook (worse) but also for U.S. equity valuations (lower).
Of course, after the U.S. accumulated another $2T deficit in FY 2026,2 “rebuilding fiscal

space” in the IMF’s words will be politically unpopular and mathematically challenging.
In our view, the biggest near-term tail risk would be a surprise move lower in growth as
budget deficits at 6% of GDP in good times leave little room for bad times. This isn’t our
base case given robust consumer spending per our internal credit and debit card data and
ongoing investment in the AI buildout and beyond. We expect these two pillars to sustain
growth in 2027.

The other good news is that, contrary to concerns, demand for Treasurys remains robust.
Yields are attractive—that was the message from October’s successful $39B 10-year
Treasury note auction. At the same time, U.S. debt boasts an array of buyers: domestic
households, the Federal Reserve, mutual funds and exchange-traded funds, money market
funds, pension funds, foreign central banks, private investors overseas, and more. Per
Exhibit 5B, whereas foreign official investors once dominated some 40% of the U.S.

Treasury market during the 2008/2009 Great Financial Crisis, their share has fallen to
12% while domestic private investors now account for a majority of holdings. Translated:
demand is more diverse than most appreciate.

Investment Implications
In our view, higher nominal and real
yields present a compelling entry point
for investors seeking income and can
provide cushion in the face of higher
rate volatility. We expect demand for
U.S. assets to remain healthy and favor
the U.S. over developed economies in
Europe facing more modest growth and
fiscal instability.

#cliffnotes #rba #australia #inflation #us #fomc #ecb #aud #usd
Cliff Notes: the source and persistence of inflation
By Ryan Wells, Illiana Jain & Elliot Clarke
11:16 October 30 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
In Australia this week, all eyes were on the September CPI. Headline inflation rose 0.8% (4.3%yr) in
the month, largely owing to a 9.4% jump in fuel prices and a seasonal surge in domestic and
international holiday travel & accommodation. The underlying inflation pulse also proved hotter than

anticipated for Q3 overall, up 1.0% (3.7%yr).

### Page 2

While some parts of the basket are seeing disinflation – housing in particular looks to be cooling faster
than expected as builders lure sales with discounts – other components are still showing persistence.
Highlighting this is market services ex volatiles, which the RBA watches closely as an indicator of price
pressures related to domestic capacity. In Q3, this metric rose 1.2% (3.2%yr), and the monthly detail
points to momentum holding up – in line with our expectations for another chunky read in Q4.

With inflation running well above the RBA’s August forecasts, and some of the energy risks flagged
then materialising, we continue to expect the Monetary Policy Board will deliver a 25bp rate hike to
4.85% in November. Beyond November, another rate hike in February is a risk, but is not currently part
of our base case.
Across the Tasman, the RBNZ this week surprised market expectations for a 25bp rate hike, deciding
instead to wait for further information before taking the next step with policy. The decision was made

4:3, with the Governor in the ‘on hold’ camp. A 25bp hike in December to 3.00% and further tightening
in 2027 (Westpac NZ forecasts a peak of 4.00% in September) seems probable; however, each
decision will depend on fuel prices, inflation expectations and evidence of second-round pricing
pressures, along with the evolution of global economic risks.
In the US, the FOMC also held rates steady at its October meeting. Based on prior comments by key
members, the decision was not a surprise, and neither were Governor Cook and Cleveland Fed

President Hammack’s dissents in favour of a 25bp hike. The Committee remains positive on economic
activity, characterising it in the statement as “expanding at a strong pace” (an upgrade from “solid” in
September). Chair Warsh also subsequently emphasised the breadth of economic growth outside of
tech-related investment (evinced by Q3 GDP the day after; see below).
The Committee refrained from spotlighting key price risks in the statement. However, inflation “remains

elevated” and the FOMC are determined to achieve a “timely” and sustainable return of annual inflation
to 2.0%yr after 6 years above target. This guidance and the “accumulation of more information will help
the Committee determine the policy stance” suggests another 25bp hike is likely in December.
Thereafter, the FOMC will remain attentive to any broadening in inflation and related risks through
2027; although, in the absence of another shock, we expect them to remain on hold.

The Q3 GDP report certainly supported Chair Warsh’s view on the breadth of growth, the 3.4%
annualised gain for GDP benefitting from a 3.8% annualised increase in household consumption,
12.7% growth in non-residential investment as well as a robust increase in government spending.
Notably though, the contribution from business investment was effectively offset by net exports’ drag
given most of the componentry related to AI infrastructure investment is imported. This is not to say that

US firms won’t benefit from the investment once complete, but simply that much of the initial return
goes to the foreign businesses who provide the necessary capital equipment.
On consumption, the monthly PCE data implies downside risks remain for the consumer, the increase
in nominal spending seen in the month requiring dissaving and, on a real basis, consumption flat in the
month following a 0.5% gain in August. Cost-of-living pressures and uncertainty over the outlook is

### Page 3

Related articles
expected to see household consumption growth average around trend over the coming year, albeit with
considerable volatility quarter to quarter. This should see PCE inflation slow back towards its 2.0%yr
medium-term target as the FOMC desires, from 3.6%yr and 3.1%yr for the headline and core
measures respectively as of September.
Across the Atlantic, the ECB also left its key policy rates unchanged in October. Of note from the
meeting communications, energy prices continue to pose upside risks for inflation, but also downside

risks for growth. "While the full effects of the energy shock are still unfolding, underlying inflation has
remained broadly stable so far and wages do not show a material response". "Financing conditions
have [also] tightened", "which is likely to weigh on inflation and growth". In the press conference,
President Lagarde noted that, between the October and December meetings, a significant amount of
new economic information will be released, allowing forecasts and risks assessments to be tested.

Based on the October meeting communications, we continue to expect a rate hike at the December
meeting but believe this will be prove to be the last hike of the cycle. Note though, the spectrum of risks
Europe faces is broad and skewed towards inflation in the near term, hence the Council’s guidance will
remain hawkish. President Lagarde also announced today that she will step down as ECB President in
February ahead of the scheduled conclusion of her term next October.

Document 339: European resilience, American vigour (2026-10-30)

Source PDF: all report pdf/European resilience, American vigour (2026-10-30).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-30
Pages: 2
Status: ok

### Page 1

Paris, 30 October 2026

EcoFlash

European resilience, American vigour

Eurozone growth continues to prove resilient, notwithstanding the volatility of the Irish figures. The Q3 data reinforce 
our forecast of 1.1% growth this year, which could even be slightly exceeded, as German and Spanish momentum shows 
no sign of fading. In France, the negative surprise (-0.1% q/q against a consensus of +0.1% q/q) stems from a new one-
off factor (the sharp fall in aerospace output caused by delays in input deliveries) and masks an encouraging rebound 
in household consumption. In the United States, growth reached its highest level since Q2 2025 (0.8% q/q), driven by 
buoyant private domestic demand.

Eurozone: The resilience of growth is confirmed 
Eurozone GDP grew by 0.4% q/q in Q3 according to Eurostat's preliminary flash estimate, above both the consensus and our 
Nowcast (0.3%). The ever-volatile Irish figures contributed 0.2 pp to this outcome. Excluding Ireland, growth (0.2% q/q) 
nonetheless remains in line with the pace of recent quarters (0.2-0.3% q/q on average). This illustrates the Eurozone's capacity 
to withstand the current shocks, thanks in particular to the strength of Spain (+0.07 pp contribution to the q/q change in Eurozone’s 
GDP) and the Netherlands (+0.04 pp), and to Germany's gradual recovery (+0.06 pp). France (-0.03 pp) and Italy (zero growth), 
by contrast, posted disappointing results, although in France's case these partly reflect temporary factors (aerospace output). The 
growth carry-over for 2026 now stands at 1.0%, which supports our forecast of 1.1% in annual average terms and even points to 
upside risk. The European Commission's October surveys remain upbeat, with a further improvement in industrial activity 
expectations. 
The German recovery continues. As anticipated by our Nowcast, real GDP grew by 0.2% q/q in Q3 (after 0.3% in Q2). 
According to Destatis, consumption (private and public) increased slightly, while investment edged down. Export momentum 
slowed markedly compared with Q2. This is Germany's fourth consecutive quarter of growth. 
Italian GDP surprised on the downside, stagnating in Q3 (against a consensus of 0.1%). According to Istat, this reflects a 
decline in value added in industry, offset by increases in services and agriculture. The positive contribution from domestic demand 
was reportedly cancelled out by a negative contribution from net trade. 
Spanish growth remained robust in Q3, in line with expectations (0.6% q/q, after 0.7% in Q2), driven mainly by domestic 
demand (0.5 pp contribution) and, to a lesser extent, by net exports (0.1 pp). Consumption remains the main engine of growth 
(+0.6% q/q for private consumption and +0.9% for public consumption). Investment rose by 0.7%. Export growth (1.3%) once 
again outpaced import growth (1.1%) this quarter. All sectors except construction recorded positive changes in value added, with 
industry and services both up 0.7%. 
France: A sharp drop in aerospace output prevents a return to growth in Q3 
GDP contracted by 0.1% q/q in Q3 (after stagnating in Q2), weighed down by a sharp fall in aerospace output (-4.5% q/q 
in the production of other transport equipment, which includes aerospace). This steep decline dragged down goods production 
(down 1.2% q/q) and explains the sharp contraction in exports (-1.7% q/q) and business investment (-0.4% q/q). Net trade thus 
subtracted 0.6 pp from GDP (imports were flat over the same period). Conversely, household consumption surprised on the 
upside (+0.4% q/q), boosted by purchases of electric vehicles (registrations up 112% y/y in Q3). Public consumption 
(+0.3% q/q) also supported growth, as did activity in market services (+0.3% q/q). Despite the sharp drop in aerospace 
output in Q3, Airbus has confirmed its delivery targets for the year (with engine supply problems expected to be resolved), pointing 
to a strong rebound in growth in Q4 (0.3% q/q according to our forecasts). Overall, growth is expected to reach 0.3% in 2026, 
before picking up to 0.8% in 2027. 
United States: Growth at a one-year high  
GDP growth picked up in Q3 to 3.4% at a seasonally adjusted annualised rate (AR; i.e. 0.8% q/q), from 2.2% in Q2 
(0.6% q/q). This is the fastest pace since Q3 2025, confirming that activity is almost immune to the energy shock. Private domestic 
demand remains particularly strong. Private non-residential investment accelerated to 12.7% AR, a performance unmatched since 
the post-Covid reopening. AI-related components are driving this figure (18.5% AR), but other types of investment are also rising 
for a second consecutive quarter (3.8% AR) after contracting uninterruptedly throughout 2025. Household consumption, steady 
at 2.5% AR in real terms, is growing faster than it did before inflation picked up again, but at the cost of a significant drop in the 
saving rate (3.8% at the end of the quarter, down from 4.4% at the end of the previous quarter). The United States nonetheless 
continues to "import its growth": net trade is weighing on GDP owing to a surge in imports (19.7% AR), driven by AI-related 
components – IT, semiconductors and telecommunications (103.8% AR).

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
All state Housing Sentiment Indexes are at weak levels.
However, November saw a bigger decline in Western
Australia (–4.8%mth) which now has the weakest housing-
related sentiment index across the major states at just 80.9.
Queensland and South Australia moved in line with the

national index. Moves were notably milder in New South
Wales (–1.7%) and especially Victoria (–0.7%) where the state
index, at 86.6, is the least pessimistic of the major states.
The sub-group detail showed a more pronounced weakening in
housing-related sentiment amongst high income earners and
consumers aged over 55 with sentiment holding about steady
amongst low-income earners, albeit at weaker overall levels.
Our November consumer sentiment survey also included

two new regular questions on consumers’ property buying
and selling intentions over the next twelve months. These
questions will continue to be run every month.
Despite the broader pessimism around housing, a relatively
high number of consumers are planning to buy in 2027.
Responses show 12.8% of consumers plan to buy a new
home over the next year. Comparable figures from Westpac’s
Home Ownership survey going back to 2019 suggest this is a
relatively firm result, up slightly since the middle of the year,

on par with the start of 2025 and above the levels reported
during the COVID pandemic in 2020-21. The result suggests
there is significant ‘latent demand’ for home purchases in
Australia, and that many buyers are just waiting for the right
opportunity. The state detail shows intentions are particularly
high in Queensland where one in five consumers is reportedly
planning to buy in 2027.
Interestingly, the positive intentions are not just around home

purchase with 11.2% of consumers reporting that they plan
to buy an investment property over the next year. Again,
comparable figures from Westpac’s Home Ownership survey
show this is up a tick compared to May and only marginally
below the 11.5% reporting intentions to buy investment
properties back in January 2025.
BULLETIN
The Westpac Housing Sentiment Index fell 2.2% to 83.2 in
November from 85.3 in October.
The Index is a composite based on housing-related sentiment

measures from the Westpac-Melbourne Institute Consumer
Sentiment survey. It already appears regularly in Westpac’s
Housing Pulse reports but will now be published on a
monthly basis. The Index has proven to be an extremely useful
predictor of turning points in housing markets, both nationally
and across states. Westpac is also introducing two new
survey questions on consumers property buying and selling
intentions that will be run every month.
The November update shows housing-related sentiment

remains remain deeply pessimistic. At 85.3, the latest Index
read is in the bottom 3% of observations since 2005, when all
components first became available. That said, it is still above the
81.4 low in late 2022 and the 78.6 registered when the COVID
pandemic hit in 2020. Housing-related sentiment was markedly
weaker during the slump in the late 1980s/early 1990s.
Component-wise, the November sentiment decline centred on
a sharp downgrade to price expectations. As detailed below,

the Westpac Housing Sentiment Index composite is based
on four sub-indexes: one tracking assessments of ‘time to buy
a dwelling’; one tracking ‘consumer house price expectations’;
one tracking consumer expectations for unemployment and a
measure of risk aversion based on consumer assessments of
the ‘wisest place for savings’.
Consumer price expectations took a big step down in
November. The Westpac–Melbourne Institute Index of
House Price Expectations recorded a steep 13.4% drop

to 99.6, marking the second biggest monthly deterioration
since the COVID period and the first net negative (sub-100)
read since November 2022. The detailed responses show
consumers are now evenly split on the outlook, just over 38%
expecting more declines, 19% expecting no change and 38%
expecting a rise (5% reported “don’t know).
At least some consumers are starting to see lower prices as
a potential buying opportunity. The ‘time to buy a dwelling’

index rose 1.3% to 89.6 in November, to be up 1.3% on a year
ago as well but still at a very weak level overall, well below
the long run average of 114.
The wider economic backdrop was also less conducive for
housing-related sentiment. Consumers continue to report
a gradual rise in job-loss fears and risk aversion remains
elevated near historic highs. Both factors are likely to be
deterring prospective home buyers.
Housing sentiment sinks but buying intentions positive

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
Conversely, just 8.2% of Australians are planning to sell a home 
over the next year. Comparable figures from Westpac’s Home 
Ownership survey suggest this is down significantly from 10.3% 
back in May and near the 7.7% low recorded at the start of 2024. 
On investment properties, 6.7% of all those surveyed reported 
plans to sell. However, only 23% of consumers reported 
owning an investment property – as a share of this sub-
group, the proportion planning to sell is just over 26%. While 
it is unclear whether this is relatively high (no comparable 
measures are available from Westpac’s Home Ownership 
survey), a third of this same sub-group reported an intention to 
buy an investment property over the next year, indicating that 
amongst investment property owners prospective buyers still 
outnumber prospective sellers. 
Overall, the consumer views on housing show a clear tension. 
On the one side, high interest rates, a continued slide in prices 
and unease about a riskier outlook for jobs and the wider 
economy are weighing heavily on housing-related sentiment. 
But against this, relatively few are planning to sell and there 
is a relatively high proportion of consumers looking to buy. 
This suggests that conditions could recover quite quickly if and 
when the macro-economic backdrop becomes less threatening 
and the market starts to stabilise.

About the Westpac Housing Sentiment Index 
The Westpac Housing Sentiment Index is a composite 
measure based on a weighted combination of four indexes 
from the Westpac–Melbourne Institute Consumer 
Sentiment survey. Two of these are ‘primary’ components 
with a higher weight that relate directly to consumer 
perceptions of housing market conditions: the Westpac–
Melbourne Institute ‘time to buy a dwelling’ index and the 
Westpac–Melbourne Institute House Price Expectations 
Index. The remaining ‘supplementary’ components, with 
lower weights, relate to consumer assessments of job 
security – the Westpac–Melbourne Institute Unemployment 
Expectations Index – and risk appetite – the Westpac Risk 
Aversion Index.
Each of these components is seasonally adjusted, converted 
to a consistent base and combined using fixed weights 
determined by historical regression analysis. Note that the 
house price expectations component is only available from 
2009 on – a re-weighted composite based on the remaining 
measures is used for earlier periods.  
The resulting composite measures provide significant insight 
into housing market conditions both nationally and at the 
individual state level. The national index has over 50yrs of 
history and a clear lead indicator relationship with a variety 
of housing market metrics. The index is particularly good at 
picking turning points in housing market turnover – correctly 
anticipating every major upswing and downturn since 1980 
with a lead of around three months (four once the timeliness 
of sentiment updates is included).

Document 390: National Bank of Hungary review_ The doves aren’t gone yet

The National Bank of Hungary kept rates unchanged in
November, but delivered a dovish twist. A rate cut was already
on the table, and improving domestic risk sentiment is creating
more room for manoeuvre. Nevertheless, we only see the next
cuts coming in late 2027 as inﬂation pressures rebuild

Peter Virovacz and Zoltán Homolya
The National Bank of Hungary, Budapest

ING's policy view: A dovish twist, but no

### Page 2

The National Bank of Hungary kept the base rate unchanged at
5.50% on 17 November, in line with widespread expectations. This
marked the third consecutive meeting with rates being kept on
hold. While the latest communication revealed somewhat more
room for monetary easing, we think the window of opportunity will
remain narrow.
Looking ahead, the exceptionally favourable domestic inﬂation
backdrop is likely to fade relatively quickly. We see inﬂation

accelerating to 3.0% by December 2026, with repricing at the start
of next year temporarily pushing price pressures closer to 3.5% in
the ﬁrst quarter of 2027. Inﬂation could then approach 4% during
the summer, largely due to base eﬀects. In our view, this
increasingly challenging inﬂation proﬁle means that any near-term
opportunity for easing may prove short-lived.
In the meantime, however, we see the latest dovish shift in
communication potentially testing the waters and gauging market

sensitivity to eventual easing, particularly in a global environment
where hawkishness still dominates. The key litmus test will be the
forint: a muted FX reaction to the dovish rhetoric would give the
central bank greater conﬁdence that some easing could be
delivered without jeopardising market stability. This means that a
possible one-and-done December rate cut has become a clear
dovish risk to our baseline.
However, we believe that any near-term window for easing would

be relatively narrow. As 2027 progresses, the main constraint on
monetary policy is likely to shift gradually from external risks
towards the domestic inﬂation outlook. With inﬂation expected to
move closer to 4% during the summer, we see little room for a
sustained easing cycle over the next six to nine months. Any near-
term easing would therefore be more likely to represent an
opportunistic move than the beginning of a conventional cutting
cycle.

A more durable easing window could open later in 2027, once
inﬂation starts to decelerate more convincingly after the summer. If
this coincides with a benign external environment and a further
Your privacy is important

decline in the risk premium on Hungarian assets, we see room for
one or two rate cuts in the fourth quarter of 2027. Our baseline
therefore sees the base rate reaching 5.00% by end-2027, followed
by a further reduction to 4.50% by end-2028.
Our three takeaways from the presser

### Page 4

Review Policy rate NBH National Bank of Hungary
Monetary policy Hungary
1. A cut was already on the table. The biggest dovish surprise
came during the press conference. The Monetary Council
considered two options: keeping rates unchanged or cutting
them, while the decision itself was not unanimous. This
suggests that the bar for easing may already be lower than
previously thought. The December Inﬂation Report will now be
particularly important, with Governor Mihály Varga explicitly

stating that favourable developments in Hungary's risk
assessment and inﬂation outlook, together with reduced
external uncertainty, could increase its room for manoeuvre.
2. Hungary's risk story has improved. Perhaps the most notable
change in the narrative is the assessment of Hungary's risk
premium. Varga highlighted the favourable market reception of
the government's ﬁscal plans and its commitment to euro
adoption as factors supporting Hungary's risk assessment and

increasing room for monetary policy. The forint has also
remained stable and is stronger than a year ago, helping to
contain inﬂationary pressures. In other words, domestic
ﬁnancial market developments are becoming less of a
constraint on monetary policy than the Monetary Council
previously considered them to be.
3. The external environment still demands caution. The greater
room for manoeuvre does not mean that the NBH is ready to
embark on an easing cycle. Energy price volatility, elevated

global yields and above-target inﬂation in major economies
continue to warrant caution. At home, inﬂation is also expected
to accelerate from its current 2% level due to fuel prices, tax
changes and other policy measures. This leaves the Monetary
Council in a familiar data-dependent position: its room for
manoeuvre has increased, but whether it can actually use that
room will depend on both the external environment and the
December inﬂation outlook.
